Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today. I hope I'm not taking up time that someone else might need more. I'm just here because... well, I guess I have a little bit of a problem with wasps.

I even gave up my gardening volunteer jobs at the church—just to be on the safe side, you know? And, um, if I hear buzzing out back, I'll usually make my husband unload things by the back porch instead of doing it myself. I just prefer to stay out of their way, if that makes sense? Oh, well... I try not to make a big fuss about it, really. I know it sounds so foolish when I say it out loud. I mean, I know a wasp is just a little insect, right? Most folks would just swat it away and go right on about their day.

But it's more... it's just the sight of them. The way they look, that aggressive way they fly, the buzzing... it just sets me off before they've even come close to me. \n\nI know it sounds silly to be so frightened just by how an insect looks and moves. A grown woman shouldn't be so bothered by a tiny little bug just flying around, should she? I hope I'm explaining this properly. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right picture of what happens. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of any other objects or situations that are similar to wasps, such as other flying insects or sharp objects?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I sew quite a bit, actually, so I'm always handling needles and pins, and I'm perfectly fine with those. \n\nI know it probably seems a bit odd that I'm so focused on just this one specific thing. Well, um... not really, no. I mean, I did mention the gardening group, but that's really the only thing. I still go to all the indoor church events, and I love going over to my friends' houses for tea or dinner, as long as we're inside. 

I'd never want to miss out on seeing people or be a bother to anyone by refusing an invitation. I just... I try not to make a big fuss about it, really. I know it sounds so foolish when I say it out loud. I mean, I know a wasp is just a little insect, right? Most folks would just swat it away and go right on about their day. \n\nBut for me... um... I don't know, if one gets anywhere near me, or if I even just think about it being close, I get this... this awful feeling. It just feels like something terrible is about to happen. I know that's probably silly to say, and I really hope you don't think I'm being overly dramatic. It's just a bit overwhelming for me, is all. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience a sudden, intense fear or panic when you see a wasp or think about being near one?\n\nPatient: Well, I really try to keep it together, I promise I do. But... yes, I suppose I do. \n\nThe second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers... it's just instant. My heart just jumps right up into my throat, and my hands start to shake. I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away, and I usually just hurry back indoors as fast as I can. I know it's completely out of proportion, and I'm so sorry if that sounds ridiculous. I really should be able to handle it better at my age, shouldn't I?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a similar reaction to other insects, like bees, or is it primarily just wasps that cause this fear?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's really just the wasps. I mean, I don't love spiders or anything, but they don't bother me the way wasps do. And bees... well, bees are quite nice, actually. They're just fuzzy little things trying to pollinate the flowers, aren't they? I can watch them work in the garden—well, when I used to garden—and be perfectly fine. \n\nIt's really just those... those wasps. With the smooth bodies and the way they dart around. And I stopped hosting my little garden days, too. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with everyone, but I just couldn't relax out there anymore. \n\nAnd at home... um... my poor husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I always used to handle myself. I just hope you don't think I'm using this as an excuse to get out of doing things. It's just that being out there became a bit too much for me to handle. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the answers you need.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the wasps themselves or about the possibility of getting stung by them?\n\nPatient: Well, I... But it's more... it's just the sight of them.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um, I suppose I have changed a few things, but really, it's nothing too drastic. I try not to let it be a burden to anyone. I did mention having to step back from the church gardening group, which... well, it's a shame, because I used to love hosting the garden days at my house. It was such a lovely way to stay connected with the ladies from church. I just tell them I'm focusing more on indoor hobbies now, so they don't think I'm being silly. 

And at home, my husband has been wonderful. He's taken over sweeping the porch and tending to the flowerbeds, things I used to do myself. I feel a bit guilty putting that on him, but he says he doesn't mind at all. I still take care of everything inside, of course! I make sure the house is spotless. I just... Oh, well, um... no, I don't think I do that. I don't really keep track of when they might be more active or anything like that. I just... I mean, as I said, I do prefer to stay inside when it's warm out, but I'm not checking the clock or avoiding specific hours.

My heart just jumps right up into my throat, and my hands start to shake. I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away, and I usually just hurry back indoors as fast as I can. I know it's completely out of proportion, and I'm so sorry if that sounds ridiculous. I really should be able to handle it better at my age, shouldn't I?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a similar reaction to other insects, like bees, or is it primarily just wasps that cause this fear?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's really just the wasps. I mean, I don't love spiders or anything, but they don't bother me the way wasps do. And bees... well, bees are quite nice, actually. They're just fuzzy little things trying to pollinate the flowers, aren't they? I can watch them work in the garden—well, when I used to garden—and be perfectly fine. \n\nIt's really just those... those wasps. With the smooth bodies and the way they dart around. I hope that doesn't sound too strange, being so afraid of one but not the other? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or places because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I have, yes. I just hope you don't think I'm using this as an excuse to get out of doing things. It's just that being out there became a bit too much for me to handle. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the answers you need.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the wasps themselves or about the possibility of getting stung by them?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, really? But mostly, it's just the wasps themselves. I mean, getting stung is dreadful, of course, and I certainly don't want that to happen. But it's more... it's just the sight of them. I sew quite a bit, actually, so I'm always handling needles and pins, and I'm perfectly fine with those. \n\nI know it probably seems a bit odd that I'm so focused on just this one specific thing. I hope it doesn't make me seem too... well, too dramatic or particular. I just want to be as helpful as I can. Does that answer your question alright?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided social gatherings or events because you thought there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I suppose I'm quite lucky in that regard, aren't I? I wouldn't want to be the sort of person who couldn't fulfill their responsibilities just because of a silly fear. I always try to do my part. Is that the kind of information you needed?\n\nDoctor: How much of your daily routine or activities have you changed because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, um, I suppose I have changed a few things, but really, it's nothing too drastic. I try not to let it be a burden to anyone. I did mention having to step back from the church gardening group, which... well, it's a shame, because I used to love hosting the garden days at my house. It was such a lovely way to stay connected with the ladies from church. I just tell them I'm focusing more on indoor hobbies now, so they don't think I'm being silly. \n\nAnd at home, my husband has been wonderful. He's taken over sweeping the porch and tending to the flowerbeds, things I used to do myself. I feel a bit guilty putting that on him, but he says he doesn't mind at all. I still take care of everything inside, of course! I make sure the house is spotless. I just... I prefer to stay indoors during the warmer months, if I can help it. Does that sound alright? I hope it doesn't make me seem too incapable.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided going outdoors during certain times of the day when wasps are more active?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, um... no, I don't think I do that. I don't really keep track of when they might be more active or anything like that. I just... I mean, as I said, I do prefer to stay inside when it's warm out, but I'm not checking the clock or avoiding specific hours. \n\nI wouldn't want to be that rigid about it, you know? That seems like it would be making too much of a fuss. If I need to go out to the car or run an errand, I just go, regardless of the time of day. I try very hard to just carry on normally when I have things to do. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like your fear of wasps is interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean... I have a very lovely life, really. My husband is so good to me, and I have my home to take care of, my baking, my cross-stitch... there's plenty to keep me busy and happy. 

I'd hate for you to think I'm just sitting around miserable or letting this ruin my days. It's really just a little... well, a little bump in the road during the summer months. I really do feel very blessed, overall. So, no, I wouldn't say it interferes with my quality of life in any big way. I just try to look on the bright side of things and count my blessings. I hope that's okay to say? I just wouldn't want to sound like I'm complaining when I have so much to be thankful for.

I'm just here because... well, I guess I have a little bit of a problem with wasps. \n\nIt sounds so silly to say out loud, doesn't it? But I've kind of gotten to the point where I sort of plan my whole summer around avoiding them. I even gave up my gardening volunteer jobs at the church—just to be on the safe side, you know? And, um, if I hear buzzing out back, I'll usually make my husband unload things by the back porch instead of doing it myself. I just prefer to stay out of their way, if that makes sense? I hope it's okay that I came in for something like this.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you see a wasp or think about being near one?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I try not to make a big fuss about it, really. I know it sounds so foolish when I say it out loud. I mean, I know a wasp is just a little insect, right? Most folks would just swat it away and go right on about their day. \n\nBut for me... um... I don't know, if one gets anywhere near me, or if I even just think about it being close, I get this... this awful feeling. It just feels like something terrible is about to happen. I know that's probably silly to say, and I really hope you don't think I'm being overly dramatic. It's just a bit overwhelming for me, is all. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience a sudden, intense fear or panic when you see a wasp or think about being near one?\n\nPatient: Well, I really try to keep it together, I promise I do. But... yes, I suppose I do. \n\nThe second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers... it's just instant. My heart just jumps right up into my throat, and my hands start to shake. I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away, and I usually just hurry back indoors as fast as I can. I know it's completely out of proportion, and I'm so sorry if that sounds ridiculous. I really should be able to handle it better at my age, shouldn't I?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a similar reaction to other insects, like bees, or is it primarily just wasps that cause this fear?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's really just the wasps. I mean, I don't love spiders or anything, but they don't bother me the way wasps do. And bees... well, bees are quite nice, actually. I just hope you don't think I'm using this as an excuse to get out of doing things. It's just that being out there became a bit too much for me to handle. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the answers you need.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the wasps themselves or about the possibility of getting stung by them?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't really keep track of when they might be more active or anything like that. I just... I mean, as I said, I do prefer to stay inside when it's warm out, but I'm not checking the clock or avoiding specific hours. \n\nI wouldn't want to be that rigid about it, you know? That seems like it would be making too much of a fuss. If I need to go out to the car or run an errand, I just go, regardless of the time of day. I try very hard to just carry on normally when I have things to do. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of wasps is interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean... I have a very lovely life, really. My husband is so good to me, and I have my home to take care of, my baking, my cross-stitch... there's plenty to keep me busy and happy. \n\nI'd hate for you to think I'm just sitting around miserable or letting this ruin my days. It's really just a little... well, a little bump in the road during the summer months. I really do feel very blessed, overall. So, no, I wouldn't say it interferes with my quality of life in any big way. I just try to look on the bright side of things and count my blessings. I hope that's okay to say? I just wouldn't want to sound like I'm complaining when I have so much to be thankful for.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your fear of wasps is something that you think about every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, I wouldn't say every day. Goodness, that sounds exhausting, doesn't it? I really try not to dwell on it. I mean, maybe during the peak of summer, if I have to go out, it might cross my mind... just a fleeting thought, really. 

But in the winter or when I'm just busy inside the house, I don't think about them at all. I wouldn't want to be the sort of person who obsesses over things like that. I try to keep my mind on more pleasant things, like what I'm making for dinner or the book I'm reading. I hope that's the right way to handle it? I just think it's better not to give these silly fears too much attention.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today. I hope I'm not taking up time that someone else might need more. I'm just here because... well, I guess I have a little bit of a problem with wasps. \n\nIt sounds so silly to say out loud, doesn't it? But I've kind of gotten to the point where I sort of plan my whole summer around avoiding them. I even gave up my gardening volunteer jobs at the church—just to be on the safe side, you know? And, um, if I hear buzzing out back, I'll usually make my husband unload things by the back porch instead of doing it myself. I just prefer to stay out of their way, if that makes sense? I hope it's okay that I came in for something like this.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you see a wasp or think about being near one?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I try not to make a big fuss about it, really. I know it sounds so foolish when I say it out loud. I mean, I know a wasp is just a little insect, right? Most folks would just swat it away and go right on about their day. \n\nBut for me... um... I don't know, if one gets anywhere near me, or if I even just think about it being close, I get this... this awful feeling. It just feels like something terrible is about to happen. I know that's probably silly to say, and I really hope you don't think I'm being overly dramatic. It's just a bit overwhelming for me, is all. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience a sudden, intense fear or panic when you see a wasp or think about being near one?\n\nPatient: Well, I really try to keep it together, I promise I do. I don't want to be one of those people who screams and makes a scene over a bug. But... yes, I suppose I do. \n\nThe second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers... it's just instant. My heart just jumps right up into my throat, and my hands start to shake. I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away, and I usually just hurry back indoors as fast as I can. I know it's completely out of proportion, and I'm so sorry if that sounds ridiculous. I really should be able to handle it better at my age, shouldn't I?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a similar reaction to other insects, like bees, or is it primarily just wasps that cause this fear?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's really just the wasps. I mean, I don't love spiders or anything, but they don't bother me the way wasps do. And bees... well, bees are quite nice, actually. They're just fuzzy little things trying to pollinate the flowers, aren't they? I can watch them work in the garden—well, when I used to garden—and be perfectly fine. \n\nIt's really just those... those wasps. With the smooth bodies and the way they dart around. I hope that doesn't sound too strange, being so afraid of one but not the other? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or places because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I have, yes. I really hate to admit it, because I know it makes me sound a bit... well, foolish, or maybe even lazy. But I’ve actually had to step back from the church gardening group. And I stopped hosting my little garden days, too. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with everyone, but I just couldn't relax out there anymore. \n\nAnd at home... um... my poor husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I always used to handle myself. I feel just terrible making him do it, I really do. I try to make up for it by doing extra chores inside, so I'm still pulling my weight, you know? I just hope you don't think I'm using this as an excuse to get out of doing things. It's just that being out there became a bit too much for me to handle. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the answers you need.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the wasps themselves or about the possibility of getting stung by them?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, really? But mostly, it's just the wasps themselves. I mean, getting stung is dreadful, of course, and I certainly don't want that to happen. But it's more... it's just the sight of them. The way they look, that aggressive way they fly, the buzzing... it just sets me off before they've even come close to me. \n\nI know it sounds silly to be so frightened just by how an insect looks and moves. A grown woman shouldn't be so bothered by a tiny little bug just flying around, should she? I hope I'm explaining this properly. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right picture of what happens. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of any other objects or situations that are similar to wasps, such as other flying insects or sharp objects?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. It really is just the wasps. I don't mind other flying things at all... well, except maybe a housefly when it's being a nuisance, but that's just annoyance, not fear. And sharp objects don't bother me either. I sew quite a bit, actually, so I'm always handling needles and pins, and I'm perfectly fine with those. \n\nI know it probably seems a bit odd that I'm so focused on just this one specific thing. I hope it doesn't make me seem too... well, too dramatic or particular. I just want to be as helpful as I can. Does that answer your question alright?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided social gatherings or events because you thought there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, I did mention the gardening group, but that's really the only thing. I still go to all the indoor church events, and I love going over to my friends' houses for tea or dinner, as long as we're inside. \n\nI'd never want to miss out on seeing people or be a bother to anyone by refusing an invitation. I just... I might ask if we're sitting on the patio, just so I know what to expect, but I don't avoid gatherings entirely. I wouldn't want anyone to think I was being difficult or unsociable. I try very hard to be a good guest and a good friend. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a job or career opportunity because it might involve being around wasps?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I've been a homemaker for most of my adult life, so I haven't really had to navigate career opportunities in that way. My husband worked, and I took care of the house and the children... well, they're grown now, of course. \n\nSo, no, I've never had to turn down a job because of it. I suppose I'm quite lucky in that regard, aren't I? I wouldn't want to be the sort of person who couldn't fulfill their responsibilities just because of a silly fear. I always try to do my part. Is that the kind of information you needed?\n\nDoctor: How much of your daily routine or activities have you changed because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, um, I suppose I have changed a few things, but really, it's nothing too drastic. I try not to let it be a burden to anyone. I did mention having to step back from the church gardening group, which... well, it's a shame, because I used to love hosting the garden days at my house. It was such a lovely way to stay connected with the ladies from church. I just tell them I'm focusing more on indoor hobbies now, so they don't think I'm being silly. \n\nAnd at home, my husband has been wonderful. I hope it doesn't make me seem too incapable.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided going outdoors during certain times of the day when wasps are more active?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, um... no, I don't think I do that. I don't really keep track of when they might be more active or anything like that. I just... I mean, as I said, I do prefer to stay inside when it's warm out, but I'm not checking the clock or avoiding specific hours. \n\nI wouldn't want to be that rigid about it, you know? Oh, my, no... a full-blown panic attack? No, I don't think so. That sounds so severe, doesn't it? I mean, I certainly get a bit flustered if one gets too close, maybe my heart beats a little faster... but I wouldn't call it a panic attack.

They're just fuzzy little things trying to pollinate the flowers, aren't they? I just hope you don't think I'm using this as an excuse to get out of doing things. It's just that being out there became a bit too much for me to handle. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the answers you need.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the wasps themselves or about the possibility of getting stung by them?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, really? But mostly, it's just the wasps themselves. I mean, getting stung is dreadful, of course, and I certainly don't want that to happen. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right picture of what happens. It really is just the wasps. I don't mind other flying things at all... well, except maybe a housefly when it's being a nuisance, but that's just annoyance, not fear. And sharp objects don't bother me either. I sew quite a bit, actually, so I'm always handling needles and pins, and I'm perfectly fine with those. \n\nI know it probably seems a bit odd that I'm so focused on just this one specific thing. I hope it doesn't make me seem too... well, too dramatic or particular. I just want to be as helpful as I can. Does that answer your question alright?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided social gatherings or events because you thought there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, I did mention the gardening group, but that's really the only thing. I still go to all the indoor church events, and I love going over to my friends' houses for tea or dinner, as long as we're inside. \n\nI'd never want to miss out on seeing people or be a bother to anyone by refusing an invitation. I just... I might ask if we're sitting on the patio, just so I know what to expect, but I don't avoid gatherings entirely. I wouldn't want anyone to think I was being difficult or unsociable. I try very hard to be a good guest and a good friend. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a job or career opportunity because it might involve being around wasps?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I really hate to admit it, because I know it makes me sound a bit... well, foolish, or maybe even lazy. But I’ve actually had to step back from the church gardening group. And I stopped hosting my little garden days, too. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with everyone, but I just couldn't relax out there anymore. \n\nAnd at home... um... my poor husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I always used to handle myself. I feel just terrible making him do it, I really do. I try to make up for it by doing extra chores inside, so I'm still pulling my weight, you know? I just hope you don't think I'm using this as an excuse to get out of doing things. It's just that being out there became a bit too much for me to handle. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the answers you need.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the wasps themselves or about the possibility of getting stung by them?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, really? But mostly, it's just the wasps themselves. I mean, getting stung is dreadful, of course, and I certainly don't want that to happen. But it's more... it's just the sight of them. The way they look, that aggressive way they fly, the buzzing... it just sets me off before they've even come close to me. \n\nI know it sounds silly to be so frightened just by how an insect looks and moves. A grown woman shouldn't be so bothered by a tiny little bug just flying around, should she? I hope I'm explaining this properly. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right picture of what happens. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of any other objects or situations that are similar to wasps, such as other flying insects or sharp objects?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. It really is just the wasps. I don't mind other flying things at all... well, except maybe a housefly when it's being a nuisance, but that's just annoyance, not fear. And sharp objects don't bother me either. I sew quite a bit, actually, so I'm always handling needles and pins, and I'm perfectly fine with those. \n\nI know it probably seems a bit odd that I'm so focused on just this one specific thing. I hope it doesn't make me seem too... well, too dramatic or particular. I just want to be as helpful as I can. Does that answer your question alright?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided social gatherings or events because you thought there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, I did mention the gardening group, but that's really the only thing. I still go to all the indoor church events, and I love going over to my friends' houses for tea or dinner, as long as we're inside. \n\nI'd never want to miss out on seeing people or be a bother to anyone by refusing an invitation. I just... I might ask if we're sitting on the patio, just so I know what to expect, but I don't avoid gatherings entirely. I wouldn't want anyone to think I was being difficult or unsociable. I try very hard to be a good guest and a good friend. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a job or career opportunity because it might involve being around wasps?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I've been a homemaker for most of my adult life, so I haven't really had to navigate career opportunities in that way. My husband worked, and I took care of the house and the children... well, they're grown now, of course. \n\nSo, no, I've never had to turn down a job because of it. I suppose I'm quite lucky in that regard, aren't I? I wouldn't want to be the sort of person who couldn't fulfill their responsibilities just because of a silly fear. I always try to do my part. Is that the kind of information you needed?\n\nDoctor: How much of your daily routine or activities have you changed because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, um, I suppose I have changed a few things, but really, it's nothing too drastic. It was such a lovely way to stay connected with the ladies from church. I just tell them I'm focusing more on indoor hobbies now, so they don't think I'm being silly. \n\nAnd at home, my husband has been wonderful. I just wouldn't want to sound like I'm complaining when I have so much to be thankful for.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of wasps is something that you think about every day?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say every day. Well... I suppose I do make a few little adjustments here and there. Nothing too extreme, I hope. But, um... I guess I have gotten to the point where I sort of plan my summer around avoiding them. Just to be on the safe side, you know?

Well... I suppose sometimes I do feel a little twinge of regret, maybe. I mean, I don't want to sound like I'm complaining or anything! But... well, like I mentioned before, I did have to step back from the church gardening group. And I used to love hosting little garden days at our house in the spring and summer. It was such a lovely way to stay connected with the ladies from the neighborhood. 

I haven't done that in a while now... I just tell everyone I'm taking a little break from entertaining. I wouldn't want them to think I'm being ridiculous about a few bugs. And at home, my husband has had to take over all the porch sweeping and the little garden tasks I used to enjoy so much. He never complains, bless his heart, but I do feel a bit bad putting it all on him. 

I just try to focus on the things I *can* still do, you know? Like baking for the church bake sale or having people over for coffee indoors. I hope that makes sense? I just think it's better to adapt and find other ways to be helpful, rather than dwelling on what I'm missing. That's a good way to look at it, isn't it?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today. I hope I'm not taking up time that someone else might need more. I'm just here because... well, I guess I have a little bit of a problem with wasps. \n\nIt sounds so silly to say out loud, doesn't it? But I've kind of gotten to the point where I sort of plan my whole summer around avoiding them. I even gave up my gardening volunteer jobs at the church—just to be on the safe side, you know? And, um, if I hear buzzing out back, I'll usually make my husband unload things by the back porch instead of doing it myself. I just prefer to stay out of their way, if that makes sense? I hope it's okay that I came in for something like this.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you see a wasp or think about being near one?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I try not to make a big fuss about it, really. I know it sounds so foolish when I say it out loud. I mean, I know a wasp is just a little insect, right? Most folks would just swat it away and go right on about their day. \n\nBut for me... um... I don't know, if one gets anywhere near me, or if I even just think about it being close, I get this... this awful feeling. It just feels like something terrible is about to happen. I know that's probably silly to say, and I really hope you don't think I'm being overly dramatic. It's just a bit overwhelming for me, is all. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience a sudden, intense fear or panic when you see a wasp or think about being near one?\n\nPatient: Well, I really try to keep it together, I promise I do. I don't want to be one of those people who screams and makes a scene over a bug. But... yes, I suppose I do. \n\nThe second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers... it's just instant. My heart just jumps right up into my throat, and my hands start to shake. I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away, and I usually just hurry back indoors as fast as I can. I know it's completely out of proportion, and I'm so sorry if that sounds ridiculous. I really should be able to handle it better at my age, shouldn't I?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a similar reaction to other insects, like bees, or is it primarily just wasps that cause this fear?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's really just the wasps. I mean, I don't love spiders or anything, but they don't bother me the way wasps do. And bees... well, bees are quite nice, actually. They're just fuzzy little things trying to pollinate the flowers, aren't they? I can watch them work in the garden—well, when I used to garden—and be perfectly fine. \n\nIt's really just those... those wasps. With the smooth bodies and the way they dart around. I hope that doesn't sound too strange, being so afraid of one but not the other? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or places because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I have, yes. I really hate to admit it, because I know it makes me sound a bit... well, foolish, or maybe even lazy. But I’ve actually had to step back from the church gardening group. And I stopped hosting my little garden days, too. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with everyone, but I just couldn't relax out there anymore. \n\nAnd at home... um... my poor husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I always used to handle myself. I feel just terrible making him do it, I really do. I try to make up for it by doing extra chores inside, so I'm still pulling my weight, you know? I just hope you don't think I'm using this as an excuse to get out of doing things. It's just that being out there became a bit too much for me to handle. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the answers you need.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the wasps themselves or about the possibility of getting stung by them?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, really? But mostly, it's just the wasps themselves. I mean, getting stung is dreadful, of course, and I certainly don't want that to happen. But it's more... it's just the sight of them. The way they look, that aggressive way they fly, the buzzing... it just sets me off before they've even come close to me. \n\nI know it sounds silly to be so frightened just by how an insect looks and moves. A grown woman shouldn't be so bothered by a tiny little bug just flying around, should she? I hope I'm explaining this properly. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right picture of what happens. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of any other objects or situations that are similar to wasps, such as other flying insects or sharp objects?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. It really is just the wasps. I don't mind other flying things at all... well, except maybe a housefly when it's being a nuisance, but that's just annoyance, not fear. And sharp objects don't bother me either. I sew quite a bit, actually, so I'm always handling needles and pins, and I'm perfectly fine with those. \n\nI know it probably seems a bit odd that I'm so focused on just this one specific thing. I hope it doesn't make me seem too... well, too dramatic or particular. I just want to be as helpful as I can. Does that answer your question alright?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided social gatherings or events because you thought there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, I did mention the gardening group, but that's really the only thing. I still go to all the indoor church events, and I love going over to my friends' houses for tea or dinner, as long as we're inside. \n\nI'd never want to miss out on seeing people or be a bother to anyone by refusing an invitation. I just... I might ask if we're sitting on the patio, just so I know what to expect, but I don't avoid gatherings entirely. I wouldn't want anyone to think I was being difficult or unsociable. I try very hard to be a good guest and a good friend. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a job or career opportunity because it might involve being around wasps?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I've been a homemaker for most of my adult life, so I haven't really had to navigate career opportunities in that way. My husband worked, and I took care of the house and the children... well, they're grown now, of course. \n\nSo, no, I've never had to turn down a job because of it. I suppose I'm quite lucky in that regard, aren't I? I wouldn't want to be the sort of person who couldn't fulfill their responsibilities just because of a silly fear. I always try to do my part. Is that the kind of information you needed?\n\nDoctor: How much of your daily routine or activities have you changed because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, um, I suppose I have changed a few things, but really, it's nothing too drastic. I try not to let it be a burden to anyone. I did mention having to step back from the church gardening group, which... well, it's a shame, because I used to love hosting the garden days at my house. It was such a lovely way to stay connected with the ladies from church. I just tell them I'm focusing more on indoor hobbies now, so they don't think I'm being silly. \n\nAnd at home, my husband has been wonderful. He's taken over sweeping the porch and tending to the flowerbeds, things I used to do myself. I feel a bit guilty putting that on him, but he says he doesn't mind at all. I still take care of everything inside, of course! I make sure the house is spotless. I just... I prefer to stay indoors during the warmer months, if I can help it. Does that sound alright? I hope it doesn't make me seem too incapable.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided going outdoors during certain times of the day when wasps are more active?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, um... no, I don't think I do that. I don't really keep track of when they might be more active or anything like that. I just... I mean, as I said, I do prefer to stay inside when it's warm out, but I'm not checking the clock or avoiding specific hours. \n\nI wouldn't want to be that rigid about it, you know? That seems like it would be making too much of a fuss. If I need to go out to the car or run an errand, I just go, regardless of the time of day. I try very hard to just carry on normally when I have things to do. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of wasps is interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean... I have a very lovely life, really. My husband is so good to me, and I have my home to take care of, my baking, my cross-stitch... there's plenty to keep me busy and happy. \n\nI'd hate for you to think I'm just sitting around miserable or letting this ruin my days. It's really just a little... well, a little bump in the road during the summer months. I really do feel very blessed, overall. So, no, I wouldn't say it interferes with my quality of life in any big way. I just try to look on the bright side of things and count my blessings. I hope that's okay to say? I just wouldn't want to sound like I'm complaining when I have so much to be thankful for.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of wasps is something that you think about every day?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say every day. Goodness, that sounds exhausting, doesn't it? I really try not to dwell on it. I mean, maybe during the peak of summer, if I have to go out, it might cross my mind... just a fleeting thought, really. \n\nBut in the winter or when I'm just busy inside the house, I don't think about them at all. I wouldn't want to be the sort of person who obsesses over things like that. I try to keep my mind on more pleasant things, like what I'm making for dinner or the book I'm reading. I hope that's the right way to handle it? I just think it's better not to give these silly fears too much attention.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of wasps has ever caused you to have a full-blown panic attack?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no... a full-blown panic attack? I wouldn't want to make a scene or lose control like that. It's really just a strong dislike, a bit of nerves... nothing as dramatic as all that. I just try to handle it as gracefully as I can. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound like I'm completely falling apart over a little bug!\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided traveling to certain places because you thought there might be wasps there?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I do make a few little adjustments here and there. Nothing too extreme, I hope. But, um... I guess I have gotten to the point where I sort of plan my summer around avoiding them. Just to be on the safe side, you know? \n\nLike, I actually gave up my volunteer garden jobs at the church. I didn't want anyone to think I was being silly, so I just told them my knees were getting a bit too stiff for all that weeding. And sometimes, if we come home and I hear buzzing near the back porch, I'll... well, I'll have my husband unload things from the car instead of me. He really doesn't mind, he's so wonderful about it. \n\nI just think it's smarter to avoid a situation if you can, rather than getting all worked up and risking a sting. That’s a sensible way to handle it, don't you think? I just try to be practical about it, so I hope that doesn't sound like I'm letting it run my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're missing out on important experiences or activities because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well... ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... I wouldn't say constantly. That makes it sound like I'm completely obsessed, doesn't it? I mean, I do like to be aware of my surroundings, just to be sensible. 

If I'm out on the patio or walking through a park, I might just... glance around a little bit, especially near flowers or trash cans where they tend to gather. But I certainly don't spend the whole time staring at the sky or jumping at every little shadow. I just think it's wise to keep an eye out, you know? Just a quick check to make sure the coast is clear so I can relax. That's fairly normal, right? I'm sure most people do that to some extent.

But... yes, I suppose I do. \n\nThe second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers... it's just instant. My heart just jumps right up into my throat, and my hands start to shake. I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away, and I usually just hurry back indoors as fast as I can. I know it's completely out of proportion, and I'm so sorry if that sounds ridiculous. I really should be able to handle it better at my age, shouldn't I?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a similar reaction to other insects, like bees, or is it primarily just wasps that cause this fear?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's really just the wasps. I mean, I don't love spiders or anything, but they don't bother me the way wasps do. And bees... well, bees are quite nice, actually. They're just fuzzy little things trying to pollinate the flowers, aren't they? I can watch them work in the garden—well, when I used to garden—and be perfectly fine. \n\nIt's really just those... those wasps. With the smooth bodies and the way they dart around. I hope that doesn't sound too strange, being so afraid of one but not the other? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or places because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I have, yes. I really hate to admit it, because I know it makes me sound a bit... well, foolish, or maybe even lazy. But I’ve actually had to step back from the church gardening group. And I stopped hosting my little garden days, too. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with everyone, but I just couldn't relax out there anymore. \n\nAnd at home... um... my poor husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I always used to handle myself. I feel just terrible making him do it, I really do. I try to make up for it by doing extra chores inside, so I'm still pulling my weight, you know? I just hope you don't think I'm using this as an excuse to get out of doing things. It's just that being out there became a bit too much for me to handle. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the answers you need.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the wasps themselves or about the possibility of getting stung by them?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, really? But mostly, it's just the wasps themselves. I mean, getting stung is dreadful, of course, and I certainly don't want that to happen. But it's more... it's just the sight of them. The way they look, that aggressive way they fly, the buzzing... it just sets me off before they've even come close to me. \n\nI know it sounds silly to be so frightened just by how an insect looks and moves. A grown woman shouldn't be so bothered by a tiny little bug just flying around, should she? I hope I'm explaining this properly. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right picture of what happens. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of any other objects or situations that are similar to wasps, such as other flying insects or sharp objects?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. It really is just the wasps. I don't mind other flying things at all... well, except maybe a housefly when it's being a nuisance, but that's just annoyance, not fear. And sharp objects don't bother me either. I sew quite a bit, actually, so I'm always handling needles and pins, and I'm perfectly fine with those. \n\nI know it probably seems a bit odd that I'm so focused on just this one specific thing. I hope it doesn't make me seem too... well, too dramatic or particular. I just want to be as helpful as I can. Does that answer your question alright?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided social gatherings or events because you thought there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, I did mention the gardening group, but that's really the only thing. I still go to all the indoor church events, and I love going over to my friends' houses for tea or dinner, as long as we're inside. \n\nI'd never want to miss out on seeing people or be a bother to anyone by refusing an invitation. I just... I might ask if we're sitting on the patio, just so I know what to expect, but I don't avoid gatherings entirely. I wouldn't want anyone to think I was being difficult or unsociable. I try very hard to be a good guest and a good friend. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a job or career opportunity because it might involve being around wasps?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I've been a homemaker for most of my adult life, so I haven't really had to navigate career opportunities in that way. My husband worked, and I took care of the house and the children... well, they're grown now, of course. \n\nSo, no, I've never had to turn down a job because of it. I suppose I'm quite lucky in that regard, aren't I? I wouldn't want to be the sort of person who couldn't fulfill their responsibilities just because of a silly fear. I always try to do my part. Is that the kind of information you needed?\n\nDoctor: How much of your daily routine or activities have you changed because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, um, I suppose I have changed a few things, but really, it's nothing too drastic. I just tell them I'm focusing more on indoor hobbies now, so they don't think I'm being silly. \n\nAnd at home, my husband has been wonderful. He's taken over sweeping the porch and tending to the flowerbeds, things I used to do myself. I feel a bit guilty putting that on him, but he says he doesn't mind at all. I still take care of everything inside, of course! I make sure the house is spotless. I just... I prefer to stay indoors during the warmer months, if I can help it. Does that sound alright? I hope it doesn't make me seem too incapable.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided going outdoors during certain times of the day when wasps are more active?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, um... no, I don't think I do that. I don't really keep track of when they might be more active or anything like that. I just... I mean, as I said, I do prefer to stay inside when it's warm out, but I'm not checking the clock or avoiding specific hours. \n\nI wouldn't want to be that rigid about it, you know? That seems like it would be making too much of a fuss. If I need to go out to the car or run an errand, I just go, regardless of the time of day. I try very hard to just carry on normally when I have things to do. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of wasps is interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean... I have a very lovely life, really. My husband is so good to me, and I have my home to take care of, my baking, my cross-stitch... there's plenty to keep me busy and happy. \n\nI'd hate for you to think I'm just sitting around miserable or letting this ruin my days. It's really just a little... well, a little bump in the road during the summer months. I really do feel very blessed, overall. I just wouldn't want to sound like I'm complaining when I have so much to be thankful for.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of wasps is something that you think about every day?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say every day. Well, um... it hasn't been a lifelong thing, if that's what you mean. I definitely haven't always been this much of a worrywart! 

It really just started... well, we were doing a bit of a remodel on the house, and the workers uncovered this wasp nest. It was quite a shock, to be honest. That was about two years and five months ago, actually. And ever since then, it's been pretty consistent. Not getting worse, I don't think! Just... lingering, I suppose. 

I guess getting startled like that just makes a person a little more cautious, don't you agree? I think anyone would be a bit more careful after a surprise like that, right? I just try to look at it as being prepared.

That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with everyone, but I just couldn't relax out there anymore. \n\nAnd at home... um... my poor husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I always used to handle myself. I feel just terrible making him do it, I really do. I try to make up for it by doing extra chores inside, so I'm still pulling my weight, you know? I just hope you don't think I'm using this as an excuse to get out of doing things. It's just that being out there became a bit too much for me to handle. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the answers you need.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the wasps themselves or about the possibility of getting stung by them?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, really? But mostly, it's just the wasps themselves. I mean, getting stung is dreadful, of course, and I certainly don't want that to happen. But it's more... it's just the sight of them. The way they look, that aggressive way they fly, the buzzing... it just sets me off before they've even come close to me. \n\nI know it sounds silly to be so frightened just by how an insect looks and moves. A grown woman shouldn't be so bothered by a tiny little bug just flying around, should she? I hope I'm explaining this properly. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right picture of what happens. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of any other objects or situations that are similar to wasps, such as other flying insects or sharp objects?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. It really is just the wasps. I don't mind other flying things at all... well, except maybe a housefly when it's being a nuisance, but that's just annoyance, not fear. And sharp objects don't bother me either. I sew quite a bit, actually, so I'm always handling needles and pins, and I'm perfectly fine with those. \n\nI know it probably seems a bit odd that I'm so focused on just this one specific thing. I hope it doesn't make me seem too... well, too dramatic or particular. I just want to be as helpful as I can. Does that answer your question alright?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided social gatherings or events because you thought there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really, no. I mean, I did mention the gardening group, but that's really the only thing. I still go to all the indoor church events, and I love going over to my friends' houses for tea or dinner, as long as we're inside. \n\nI'd never want to miss out on seeing people or be a bother to anyone by refusing an invitation. I just... I might ask if we're sitting on the patio, just so I know what to expect, but I don't avoid gatherings entirely. I wouldn't want anyone to think I was being difficult or unsociable. I try very hard to be a good guest and a good friend. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a job or career opportunity because it might involve being around wasps?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I've been a homemaker for most of my adult life, so I haven't really had to navigate career opportunities in that way. My husband worked, and I took care of the house and the children... well, they're grown now, of course. \n\nSo, no, I've never had to turn down a job because of it. I suppose I'm quite lucky in that regard, aren't I? I wouldn't want to be the sort of person who couldn't fulfill their responsibilities just because of a silly fear. I always try to do my part. Is that the kind of information you needed?\n\nDoctor: How much of your daily routine or activities have you changed because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, um, I suppose I have changed a few things, but really, it's nothing too drastic. I try not to let it be a burden to anyone. I did mention having to step back from the church gardening group, which... well, it's a shame, because I used to love hosting the garden days at my house. It was such a lovely way to stay connected with the ladies from church. I just tell them I'm focusing more on indoor hobbies now, so they don't think I'm being silly. \n\nAnd at home, my husband has been wonderful. He's taken over sweeping the porch and tending to the flowerbeds, things I used to do myself. I feel a bit guilty putting that on him, but he says he doesn't mind at all. I still take care of everything inside, of course! I make sure the house is spotless. I just... I prefer to stay indoors during the warmer months, if I can help it. Does that sound alright? I hope it doesn't make me seem too incapable.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided going outdoors during certain times of the day when wasps are more active?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, um... no, I don't think I do that. I don't really keep track of when they might be more active or anything like that. I just... I mean, as I said, I do prefer to stay inside when it's warm out, but I'm not checking the clock or avoiding specific hours. \n\nI wouldn't want to be that rigid about it, you know? That seems like it would be making too much of a fuss. If I need to go out to the car or run an errand, I just go, regardless of the time of day. I try very hard to just carry on normally when I have things to do. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of wasps is interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean... I have a very lovely life, really. My husband is so good to me, and I have my home to take care of, my baking, my cross-stitch... there's plenty to keep me busy and happy. \n\nI'd hate for you to think I'm just sitting around miserable or letting this ruin my days. It's really just a little... well, a little bump in the road during the summer months. I really do feel very blessed, overall. So, no, I wouldn't say it interferes with my quality of life in any big way. I just try to look on the bright side of things and count my blessings. I hope that's okay to say? I just wouldn't want to sound like I'm complaining when I have so much to be thankful for.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of wasps is something that you think about every day?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say every day. Goodness, that sounds exhausting, doesn't it? I really try not to dwell on it. I mean, maybe during the peak of summer, if I have to go out, it might cross my mind... just a fleeting thought, really. \n\nBut in the winter or when I'm just busy inside the house, I don't think about them at all. I wouldn't want to be the sort of person who obsesses over things like that. I try to keep my mind on more pleasant things, like what I'm making for dinner or the book I'm reading. I hope that's the right way to handle it? I just think it's better not to give these silly fears too much attention.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of wasps has ever caused you to have a full-blown panic attack?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no... a full-blown panic attack? No, I don't think so. That sounds so severe, doesn't it? I mean, I certainly get a bit flustered if one gets too close, maybe my heart beats a little faster... but I wouldn't call it a panic attack. \n\nI always try to just take a deep breath and keep my composure. I wouldn't want to make a scene or lose control like that. It's really just a strong dislike, a bit of nerves... nothing as dramatic as all that. I just try to handle it as gracefully as I can. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound like I'm completely falling apart over a little bug!\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided traveling to certain places because you thought there might be wasps there?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I do make a few little adjustments here and there. Nothing too extreme, I hope. But, um... I guess I have gotten to the point where I sort of plan my summer around avoiding them. Just to be on the safe side, you know? \n\nLike, I actually gave up my volunteer garden jobs at the church. I didn't want anyone to think I was being silly, so I just told them my knees were getting a bit too stiff for all that weeding. And sometimes, if we come home and I hear buzzing near the back porch, I'll... well, I'll have my husband unload things from the car instead of me. He really doesn't mind, he's so wonderful about it. \n\nI just think it's smarter to avoid a situation if you can, rather than getting all worked up and risking a sting. That’s a sensible way to handle it, don't you think? I just try to be practical about it, so I hope that doesn't sound like I'm letting it run my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're missing out on important experiences or activities because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose sometimes I do feel a little twinge of regret, maybe. I mean, I don't want to sound like I'm complaining or anything! But... well, like I mentioned before, I did have to step back from the church gardening group. And I used to love hosting little garden days at our house in the spring and summer. It was such a lovely way to stay connected with the ladies from the neighborhood. \n\nI haven't done that in a while now... I just tell everyone I'm taking a little break from entertaining. I wouldn't want them to think I'm being ridiculous about a few bugs. And at home, my husband has had to take over all the porch sweeping and the little garden tasks I used to enjoy so much. He never complains, bless his heart, but I do feel a bit bad putting it all on him. \n\nI just try to focus on the things I *can* still do, you know? Like baking for the church bake sale or having people over for coffee indoors. I hope that makes sense? I just think it's better to adapt and find other ways to be helpful, rather than dwelling on what I'm missing. That's a good way to look at it, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly checking your surroundings for wasps when you are outdoors?\n\nPatient: Well... I wouldn't say constantly. That makes it sound like I'm completely obsessed, doesn't it? I mean, I do like to be aware of my surroundings, just to be sensible. \n\nIf I'm out on the patio or walking through a park, I might just... glance around a little bit, especially near flowers or trash cans where they tend to gather. But I certainly don't spend the whole time staring at the sky or jumping at every little shadow. I just think it's wise to keep an eye out, you know? Just a quick check to make sure the coast is clear so I can relax. That's fairly normal, right? I'm sure most people do that to some extent.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this fear of wasps consistently over the years or has it varied in intensity?\n\nPatient: Well, um... it hasn't been a lifelong thing, if that's what you mean. I definitely haven't always been this much of a worrywart! \n\nIt really just started... well, we were doing a bit of a remodel on the house, and the workers uncovered this wasp nest. It was quite a shock, to be honest. That was about two years and five months ago, actually. And ever since then, it's been pretty consistent. Not getting worse, I don't think! Just... lingering, I suppose. \n\nI guess getting startled like that just makes a person a little more cautious, don't you agree? I think anyone would be a bit more careful after a surprise like that, right? I just try to look at it as being prepared.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
